ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e2051	Additional question? - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e2067	Can you imagine, Minister, that pension expectations are just as valuable as savings books, and that civil law is treated and divided in the same way as divorce?
ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e2082	Federal Minister, please.
ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e2097	This is a multi-layered problem. We must see that this can only be seen in the context of marital property law and also in the basic regulation of the right of divorce. In connection with the matrimonial property law – as the comparison with Germany and other European countries was obvious – there was the assumption that there was a community of achievements: a model that was not required by the expert group for Austria. It would also be a certain disparity to divide only careers – that is, my opinion – and not generally to consider the question of total assets, insofar as, in the lower-income social strata, the pension is essentially on social security, while in the case of higher-income social classes, in particular for entrepreneurs, it is essentially in other areas, especially in the company itself. I didn't think it was right not to see that altogether, because otherwise the "little man" would have to split his old age pension. For example, the entrepreneur leaves the company out of the division and thus does not contribute to the pension scheme of the other person. This is a very complex problem which must be seen from all sides. I would like to highlight very briefly the second aspect, which is very important here: Wherever there is the pension division, there is the indebted-independent right of divorce. It would be, if I remember the discussion here in the house, perhaps not entirely consistent, simply to say: In principle, the principle of indebtedness applies with regard to divorce, but pension expectations are divided without regard to debt. – So there is a wealth of problems that need to be further discussed and whose solution depends essentially on how to shape the social security status of the non-working woman within the marriage.

In addition, the hardware and software used in connection with the manufacturer companies is tested for its year-2000 suitability. As far as problems arise in individual cases, measures have been taken to remedy them. Regardless of this year-2000 problem, the so-called redesign infrastructure project of the IT deployment will in principle be revised in the area of the department, which has provided an opportunity and will also allow older components to be replaced and the programmes used to be up-to-date, while also ensuring the Year 2000 capability. Infrastructural facilities, i.e. devices with computer chips, will be reviewed in cooperation with specially trained officials of the Federal Ministry of Economic Affairs. These are essentially safety systems, heating, lifts, power supply et cetera. With regard to the year 2000 problem, we pay particular attention to the area of criminal proceedings, which is particularly concerned with ensuring that the security and supply facilities work. There are, in principle, disaster, emergency and alarm plans, which have now also been supplemented with regard to this problem, which may arise if what we rather do not accept, on 1 January 1989. There are difficulties in January 2000. The preparatory work for the state that we think it is all checked, more could not be done, will be completed at the beginning of November.

ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e3641	There have also been fundamental developments in liability law, not always in comparison with foreign countries. But above all, we must bear in mind that in Austria we have a situation where, in particular, damage to the person is largely taken up by the public or by the social security institutions – a condition that has the advantage for the injured, that the social security provider does not ask for the damage after a co-indebtedness of the injured person. In principle, however, you are right that in the teaching and also in the practitioners of the previous principle, which governs the Austrian right of compensation for damages, there is: In principle, the damage suffered by the injured shall be borne unless a legal provision grants him compensation for damages, is no longer a matter of course today, and a change of consciousness has taken place as regards the basic structure. This would be the starting point to introduce a uniform system of compensation, which is also very fragmentedly regulated. This is, of course, a mammoth project, which must first be prepared scientifically and, above all, should be examined for its economic consequences. So I'd think that's a project that has another horizon than one or two legislative periods.

The same is true of pornography. There must not at all be a real act of fornication, but it is enough if only the impression that such an act is given is awakened. If there is a real act of fornication, then a crime is realized, which is not disversionable. Secondly, in principle; we have discussed this several times here – this does not mean that those offences, for which individual judges or district courts are responsible, are always and in any case subject to the division, but there are debt, prevention and follow-up-related conditions of application. Only if the debt is low if there are no general or special concerns, if the situation allows for sacrifice, will there be a disversional measure. As a rule, none of the offences you mentioned today or in the past will result in a disversional settlement.

ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e5310	Mr President. Federal Minister! High house! Let me explain why we are convinced. Let's start with the analysis of the state of the state. We have excellent scientists, excellent universities and institutes with a worldwide reputation. But there are also some special features at our universities. First: Our graduates need about two years longer than those in most comparable countries. Secondly, We have a high drop-out quota; too many do not finish their studies. Thirdly, We have a growing number of working students. Fourth: International and, above all, European development goes to three-way studies with Bachelor's, Master's and Doctor's studies. This was clearly defined by the new German framework school law, by the Sorbonne Declaration and lastly by the Bologna Declaration, which at least 29 Ministers of Education and Science signed in Europe. That's where the direction is. The Studiengesetz 1997 was undoubtedly a milestone. Since then, however, the development has continued very rapidly. The study commissions decide whether they want to stay in the two-member system with the Master or Diploma Engineering Title and the subsequent doctoral degree, or whether they want to switch to the new three-member degree with Bachelor, Magister or Diploma Engineering Title and PhD titles. And because it is also important to position this new conclusion with real responsibility, we have provided a very qualified body for the issue of acceptance of this conclusion on the labour market in order to issue an opinion, namely the Advisory Board on Economic and Social Affairs. What is the point of this threefold study? – First of all, it is up to development not to scratch everything and everyone over a comb, but to address the situation on the labour market much more differentiated and thus much more efficiently. This is better with a three-member study than with a two-member study. This differentiation is a characteristic of European development. Secondly, There is an earlier opportunity for graduates to enter the labour market with an academic degree, which is fully in line with the European recognition directive for higher education studies. But if someone doesn't want to, but wants to continue to study, he can do it completely. He can, I would like to maintain this clearly, continue this study as a magisterial and doctoral degree. There is no intention on our part to limit family aid or the funding of studies in any way. Thirdly, On the basis of this basic scientific training, much more differentiated than previously on the highly differentiated claims on the labour market – whether in private companies or in the public service – can be received much better than previously possible with the two-member study. Finally, this three-way system is also facing a trend, namely that of lifelong learning. In other words, after the Bachelor's degree, after a few years of professional activity, you can return to the university, in a year or two, you can complete the Master's degree and later also your doctoral degree. Appropriate professional activity: We also require universities to take better account of the fact that around 50 percent of students are working in some form of professional activity – for whatever reason. Students can report this, and the university is obliged to take this into account when defining the courses and exams offered. Let me address three more details of this draft law. For the first time, we believe that higher education schools – a very important pillar of our education system – are recognised to the extent that the chairman of the study committee can and should count on examinations from this field. We also take into account the need for internationalisation through the introduction of ECTS points, and we have also provided the opportunity for our university courses to participate in international courses and to be awarded the corresponding titles there. The last legislative period – Mr Graf mentioned this very commendably – was very busy. We have passed a number of important laws up to the reform of the art universities, the continuation of development in the technical colleges and the close implementation of the UOG. A number of important education and higher education policies have been set up. It is essential that private universities offer studies which lead to academic degrees that are at least three years of full-time studies, that human resources, spatial and material resources are guaranteed and that the principle of freedom of science and teaching is observed. The titles given to these educational institutions receive the addition "the private university". For this purpose, an accreditation board has been set up to carry out accreditation. This includes eight members appointed by the Federal Government, four on a proposal from the rector's conference. This Accreditation Council shall act completely independently and wisely. [[Abg. Dr Gredler: All right! Completely!]] That is to say, one has found a solution similar to that found at the University Council, and that was a good solution. It has certainly been moving a lot in recent years at the universities. Some new tertiary institutions have emerged, for example through the University of Applied Sciences Law 1993. At present, there is the interest of some foreign universities to offer study programmes in Austria as well. This can certainly be a positive addition to the education offered by the Austrian universities, and new access to education and training for students can be created. However, it should be said critically that free university access on detours could be undermined by tuition fees at private universities, so that in the future someone could come to the idea of introducing tuition fees at state universities, which we strongly reject. It should also be feared that two classes of, in this case, socially privileged students will arise and that competition for state universities could arise through the better financial donation of private universities. So this is a small law. There was in itself only a small need for regulation, as at present few providers are currently on the market. Due to the high tuition fees, these universities represent only a narrow offer for a few privileged students who can afford these high fees. That is, the law will have little latitude effect. Whether the institutional anchoring of private universities in Austria was therefore necessary or desirable, may at least be critically commented. The future will show whether the offers will be an enrichment of the Austrian educational landscape or whether they will only serve the purpose of a wanted elite.

We have brought the right of organisation to a uniform level for all universities. We have finally raised the art universities to the same level as all other universities in the interest of students, by establishing a uniform right of organisation for all universities. We have established a uniform law of study for all universities by proposing the university study law in 1997 and adopting it here. It is already apparent from the first new study plans introduced on this basis that a quality leap could be achieved. We have now extended this base to the art universities and have also given these universities the opportunity to redesign their study plans in accordance with the same system, mainly to contribute to the reduction of the studies and, moreover, to focus the study plans more closely on the needs of society. That is why we have provided under the University Study Act a right of assessment of those who are either employers of the graduates of these studies in the future or who themselves have to represent such graduates as employee organisations. What has so far been achieved in the Ministry of Science in non-repudiation studies shows that universities have not only understood this challenge but are also able to meet this challenge to the fullest extent. It is about giving universities and, in the narrowest sense, the study commissions the opportunity – if they think this makes sense! – instead of offering a three-way study. As with all other study plans, it is also planned in this case that a review procedure should be carried out before the university definitely decides and before finally the Minister also has to decide. I think that this alone is already a broad assurance that not from jux and tollery suddenly quite different studies are offered than those you need. I think that the offer to use new possibilities, if necessary, is exactly the method which is also in line with what is stated in the Bologna Declaration, namely that the respective national culture of study development is taken into account and that it is not intended to bring the content into line, but that certain elements should be made comparable in the structure, and this is quite a careful way. High house! Finally, the accreditation law is also under discussion here today. And this law, too, is not the bad event that some fear in it, but it is a step that brings quality control for something that already exists today. We should not close our eyes to the fact that there are institutions called "Private University" today. Only they currently live completely unhapped and free. What this accreditation law is about is to introduce a minimum level of quality control for this sector. Because we do not want students under the title of "university" to be lured into a expensive study of which they have nothing but costs afterwards. The second concerns the prohibition of the transport of mother animals in the last fifth of pregnancy and at the time of lactation. On the one hand, this should take full account of the labelling obligation and, on the other, of the animal welfare principle. Thus, contrary to the EU Directive, the relatively liberal derogation provided for in the legislative text from the prohibition of animal testing in the cosmetic field is reversed, thus correcting a serious deficiency. ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e7753	Next speaker is Mrs. Dr. Pittermann. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e7775	Mr President. Federal Minister! High house! The Austrian animal testing law 1989 is one of the most modern and advanced internationally. The 1986 directive regulates animal testing matters within the EU. Even if the implementation of this Directive was not yet necessary in 1989, the Austrian legislator was already trying to take account of the provisions of this EU Directive in animal testing legislation. In addition, this law lays down the substantive and human conditions for the implementation of animal testing in many respects compared to the EU Directive. Animal testing is limited under Austrian law only if no alternative methods are available. This amendment to the Animal Test Act establishes additional provisions for the complete and dubious implementation of the EU Animal Experimental Directive and the conditions for a uniform EU statistics on the comparability of statistical data. Similarly, fear, pain and suffering of the animals must be minimized. The animals must be taken from breeding facilities and marked. This amendment to the Animal Testing Act prohibits animal testing for cosmetics in principle, and this ban will be put into effect early against the EU-wide ban in Austria. Animal tests are only valid for certain indications and must be approved. The EU statistics include the number and species of experimental animals and the origin of EU or Member States of the Council of Europe. It is important to consider animals as living beings and not as things. I personally can hardly imagine that animal tests are totally avoidable. At all medical congresses, research results and animal testing are always reported. Animal experiments are the result of a weighting of goods for humans and animals in medical and pharmaceutical research and development for the defence of hazards to health and the environment and, according to the current state of science, still indispensable. Austria is exemplary in its animal testing scheme. According to Austrian animal testing statistics, in Austria, the number of animals used has decreased from 1991 to 1998 to less than a third. The reasons for this are the strict regulations, an increasingly conscious and responsible attitude of scientists, researchers and animal testing institutions, a restrictive attitude of all competent authorities in the approval of animal testing and promotion of replacement methods, with the Ministry of Science spending high funds on it and the sustainable persecution of the three "R", reduction, reflection and replacement, in accordance with the Animal Test Law. We Social Democrats are also absolutely in favour of a federal animal welfare law. In this legislative period, however, it was not feasible despite our desire. It's true: If animal protection is not observed – we have seen this in the example of Great Britain – if animals are not considered to be fair, then this can have serious consequences not only for animals but also for humans, and therefore we will continue to agree on and support federal uniform animal protection. The fact that a legal amendment was not possible at the moment is unfortunate, but it is fact. I agree that the number of animal experiments to be kept as low as possible must be prevented from suffering. The priority for us Social Democrats is always human. That is why we are happy to agree to this government proposal.

ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e7918	Mr President. Mr. Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! High house! This new law contains a very far-reaching conservation of the animal world, and I believe that it was high time to protect the animals from cosmetic testing purposes. However, I would also like to use this opportunity to make some ethically critical considerations of animal testing in the framework of medical research. ==References== The principle of "nihil nocere" was in medicine at all: above all, it was not harmful. But there have always been outsiders who have tried to get nature's secrets through experiments with animals, raids, war criminals or prisoners. Only in the middle of the 19th century. In 1856, in his book "Introduction into Experimental Medicine", Claude Bernard put the animal experiment as a test stone for all medical research. At the time, unimaginable things were demanded – and also done by him. Dogs and cats were nailed and tied without anaesthesia and performed physiological experiments and operations. – Today, the methods have become more modern and perhaps less painful, but still painful. Today, it is almost impossible to demonstrate whether animal experiments are essential for knowledge in medicine or clinical research. Undoubtedly, since Claude Bernard, the experimentators and their followers have tried again and again to give any scientific knowledge of the experiments with animals. With the animal experiments, natural science medicine came into the center and man in his wholeness, body and soul, into the background. We really have to ask ourselves whether this is a curse or a blessing for us. The scientific approaches actually shifted the focus of medicine away from the idea of healing to the repair of an organ. This is documented by saying "the stomach" in rooms 7, "the galle" in rooms 6. This actually characterizes the human being by means of his organ – and not by his entire personality. In those days the soul of man was also thrown away with the disregard of the creature. For the supposed progress, one has lost fear of the Creator and the creature animal and man. There must be a critical ethical assessment of science. Today it is known that animal experiments are not always or even very rarely transferable to humans. According to the 1995 UN report, 8 500 animal-tested drugs had to be withdrawn from the market. Adverse reactions have only been detected in humans. In this context, I would just like to refer to the administration of Contergan. Worldwide, estimated 210 million animals have to die annually from animal testing. The Animal Test Act has the exclusive objective of reducing the number of animal experiments and promoting replacement methods. In fact, since 1988 the number of animal experiments in Austria has been significantly reduced. If there were still 482,000 in 1991, this figure was reduced to 168 000 in 1997. But that's still too much! The result of an animal experiment is generally only valid for the animal, only for this experimental animal and the tested preparation on the animal! All conclusions derived from them for man are only interpretations, hypotheses and assumptions. In this light, it is serious to ask whether these tests are acceptable and ethically acceptable. Don't you pretend false security for man? Does man have the right to raise an enormously large mountain of tormented, mistreated and killed animals for the research of his diseases and for the development of cures? – That’s a question we need to ask ourselves. Increasingly, in almost all areas of medical studies, in vitro methods are used, tests in test tubes are carried out. These are studies with pain-free materials: Microorganisms, tissue samples, cell material in nutritional solutions. For example, isolated but viable heart cells are looking for cardiological drugs. Hepatic cells of humans and animals allow precise statements on the reduction of drugs, chemicals and so on. Suitable in vitro procedures are meaningful, representative, reproducible, cheaper and faster, but especially ethically acceptable. As is the case with surgical procedures, I do not know exactly, I do not know whether animal experiments are necessary in this area. I could imagine, however, that computer simulations can replace a number of attempts. But especially in cosmetic products, animal experiments are certainly not appropriate, and this is regulated today by this new law. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e9153	Mr President. High house! I should like to follow up on Mrs Moser's statement. (Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) The aim of this law was not to liberalise the Austrian rail market, so it does not mean liberalisation law, but it is called rail transport regulation law. The opening of the Austrian rail network for competition has been decided here in the House a year and a half ago, on the occasion of the decision on the Railways Adjustment Act at the end of 1997. Since then, there have been legal conditions for other railways to operate on Austria's rails under the conditions of reciprocity. - You're not perceived, that's true. The second question is: What ways are open to create a reasonable framework within which competition can take place under some sort of fair conditions? In principle – and the liberal side is right – there are two approaches to this. One point of approach is that which is being discussed within the European Union and which does not have our approval, namely the demolition of railway undertakings into an infrastructure side and a sales area. We don't think that's a sensible way. However, we believe it is necessary to create conditions for competition to ensure that this competition takes place fairly. That's why we're going the way of a regulator. We have not gone the way that has been taken in the field of telecommunications, namely the path of asymmetric opening up of the market, because, unlike the telecommunications industry, Mr Firlinger, the railway market is not a highly attractive, highly profitable market, where everyone is concerned that they finally arrive. [[Intrusion of the Abg. May. Swirl]] It is rather to be feared that there will be a few very attractive routes, but on the other routes that we want to maintain the infrastructure in the interest of providing the population with equivalent means of transport and in the interest of transport of goods for which it is appropriate on the road instead of on the road, we must ensure that railways operate. This also requires, among other things, a different approach to regulation, a different approach to liberalisation. Therefore, a clear commitment from our side to this path, to a regulatory authority that can ensure that access to the Austrian rail transport system is free of discrimination and fair, but against a form of Hurra liberalisation and market opening, which only serves to ensure that others take the attractive routes and the ÖBB remain on the less attractive but in the interest of the population so to speak. I believe that with the step we are taking today, we are taking a good course, an Austrian path that also takes into account the interests of taxpayers – because they are the interests of the company ÖBB – and the interests of employees in this company who have to provide the services in the interests of the customers of the railway. In this sense, I would ask for as broad a consent as possible.

Public transport costs around 27 billion schilling. Only 7.5 billion of them pay passengers. That is, we have a cost coverage of around 27 percent. 11 billion come from the federal government, the rest from the countries, municipalities and the family compensation fund. The Federal Government will continue to pay its contribution to public passenger transport in the amount of 11 billion Schilling per year in the next ten years, thus ensuring the basic supply of this public transport. This is a very important result of these negotiations. The fact that this could be laid down in this law and thus also the basic provision of costs could be avoided on the countries and communities was ultimately a decisive reason for the federal, state and local authorities to agree on this matter of law. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] New additional transport services beyond basic services will be supported by the federal government by up to 50 percent up to an annual maximum of 680 million Schilling when countries and municipalities cover another 50 percent of the costs. That is to say, an additional offer will also be made, and the federal government and countries must jointly finance 50 percent of this offer. For the next five years, the federal government will also receive a corresponding degressive charge of the previous losses of bus traffic. At the beginning, 115 million schillings are available, and these federal funds can be used by the countries in their own responsibility for local transport. Since the settlement of new establishments is often linked to a noticeable increase in traffic, cities and municipalities will be entitled to remove from new traffic agents, i.e. from farms with more than 10,000 square metres of area where increased customer power is expected, such as leisure centres or shopping centres, where there is a great deal of transport incentives to remove a corresponding local transport tax, a traffic connection charge intended to cover the costs of setting up public transport to these establishments. To what extent this is going to be effective, ladies and gentlemen, we must certainly wait. This is an empowerment of the communities. You can do it, but you don't have to. In this way we also rely on the individual situation of the communities and also on the fact that they themselves are able and willing to solve this traffic problem that is due to them. Finally, it is also up to the community to express the corresponding clear political will as to whether it intends to take such a measure. We know, of course, that the effectiveness can be limited, especially because of the competitive conditions of operations. As a result of these conditions of competition, some municipalities are likely to refrain from collecting such a traffic connection charge. However, we believe that this measure should be in the discretion and autonomy of the community, and it should be used accordingly. Ladies and gentlemen! Public transport needs more money. It's quite clear to us. We have therefore also drawn up a resolution on this subject, so that representatives of the countries, the Federal Republic of Germany and the Federal Republic of Germany can quickly set up a working group to draw up, in preparation for the next financial compensation, a concrete proposal for a financing cover including a priority line for the investment needs of public transport. Ladies and gentlemen, this law regulates a lot of things – they have already been listed today – but additional and necessary infrastructure financing is not provided by this law. But this is necessary. This is a task of the next legislative period. I would remind you that Germany's own financing law allows local transport to be financed in municipalities and cities, both from dedicated resources of mineral oil tax and from budget resources. We must also come to a similar solution in Austria. I believe that, over the next legislative period, it must also be possible to create an own financing law which regulates the criteria for funding and the allocation of resources available for public transport in the municipalities, in the cities, in the agglomerations. It is also a question of making new investments, such as the construction or extension of important road transport roads to the public transport network, the introduction of special lanes for bus buses, the promotion of traffic control systems, the construction of transit parking spaces, the construction and expansion of trams, high-speed railways, underground railways, central bus stations and stops, and the implementation of appropriate acceleration measures. And, of course, the supply of additional vehicles, i.e. railway vehicles and cetera, needs to be improved in general. Only if the additional funding is realized during the next legislative period, and with it we are not only developing or maintaining the existing offer, but really a new additional offer for public transport, will it be possible to tackle the overarching individual traffic in the conurbation area, congestion and air pollution and also to solve the transport problems of the future in an environmentally sound manner. This is a task ahead of us, which we must prescribe in the next legislative period.

That is why – I remember many debates about this here in the House – a combined system of money and services has been introduced and defined in the Treaty, in the agreement between the federal government and countries in accordance with Article 15a of the Constitutional Law. Accordingly, joint measures should be taken for those in need of care by countries with mandatory minimum standards of performance and quality criteria. I do not wish to repeat, as has already been pointed out, that some of the quality criteria are defined in this Treaty. This is the result of negotiations with the countries. ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e12777	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen of the House! Transport has undergone constant change in recent years. We must note that traffic density in particular has increased enormously. I would like to prove this by means of a few figures. In 1990, the motor vehicle stock was 440 pieces per 1 000 inhabitants. Eight years later, in 1998, we must increase by around 25 percent. We currently hold – with increasing trend – at 550 motor vehicles per 1 000 inhabitants. But, ladies and gentlemen, also the numbers of counterparts from Lower Austria to Vienna show a similar picture. In 1991, we were able to record 122 500 commuters from Lower Austria across the city border to Vienna, of which 80 000 were motor vehicles – a percentage of about 65 percent – and only 42 500 commuters – about 35 percent – took public transport. This means that we need a strong shift towards public transport. The increasing cost of financing, but also the demand of countries and communities for a customer-oriented offer, as well as the changed framework conditions resulting from the accession to the European Union, make it necessary to create new rules. After tough negotiations, this law has now succeeded in creating the foundations or the conditions for a reorganisation of public and regional transport. From this point of view, I should like to thank all the ladies and gentlemen who have contributed to this law: whether the officials in the ministries, the country representatives – in particular, I would like to point out Salzburg and Lower Austria – or the private providers. And, Mr President, it was in the end your attempt to achieve this law, but also the representatives of the Municipal and Municipal Governments have helped to find a solution. Under this new law, countries and communities will be increasingly involved in planning in the future. Especially in rail passenger transport, in road transport, but also in transport associations, closer cooperation is needed in the future. The efficient planning and prevention of parallel transport should also lead to a reduction in roadmap kilometres. The basic offer is secured by the Ministry for the years 1999 and 2000 and, we hope, there will also be cost savings. These financial resources, which are to be released, are intended to be used for quality assurance measures. I would just like to mention some quality criteria: user-friendly card output devices, the good accessibility of stops as well as the shortest possible travel times and overtime. My venerations Ladies and gentlemen! It is also gratifying that the student and apprentice journeys are also included in this scheme, with a uniform rate for students in the course of 2000 and 2001. However, if we want to meet future public and regional transport requirements, of course we need additional financial resources. However, the law now in force does not guarantee these financial resources, and I therefore have the right to submit the following request: [[Quoted print version removed]] Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! This law provides a reasonable basis. We will therefore, of course, be happy to give our approval to this draft law.

ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e13233	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! Austria's transport policy in the enlarged Europe is in the field of tension between economic orientation on the one hand and security and environmental protection on the other. Transport policy must therefore be based on the objectives of environmental protection and the protection of life and health. The aim must therefore be to create a transport infrastructure that covers all the needs and ensures the best possible cost-effective and environmentally friendly relocation of transport. [[Abg. May. Peter: What do you need a new tax for, Mr. Dietachmayr? It's going without it!]] In principle, the Federal Act to be adopted today is intended to bring about a restructuring of public passenger transport, i.e. to achieve a consistent transition to the customer principle in the case of non-individual services, while ensuring the financing. Now to the starting position: Currently, the financing of public passenger and regional transport offers a very different picture. In at least ten different species, around 18 billion schillings per year are spent on public transport operations: from the federal government to the Länder to the municipalities. Due to the partly different funding rates and funding measures, the Federal Ministry of Science and Transport has commissioned a study at the Institute of Financial Science and Infrastructure Policy at the Technical University of Vienna for a more efficient model of the Federal's participation in the transport associations and for a redesign that illustrates this problem. Building on this study, individual provisions have now been included in this law. It is, as I believe, a few very important markers that will be set for future transport policy today. We are committed to shifting traffic flows towards energy-saving and environmentally friendly modes and forms of transport. We are in favour of developing and further modernising and attracting public passenger transport. We therefore advocate a financing system that meets the transport needs of the environment and of the environment and helps to overcome today's unfair financial competition between different modes of transport. But transport funding needs cost transparency, and that is an essential point! Public transport does not need a schilling of the taxpayer as a gift, ladies and gentlemen. Public transport is more than sufficiently financed if, in addition to its normal proceeds, it receives a fair remuneration of the benefits it brings to road users, consumers, economic operators and taxpayers by avoiding external follow-up costs of private car transport. This is a very essential fact. There are a number of measures, and there are also quality criteria in these provisions. It is, as I think, time to actually implement these models where they have not yet been implemented. These include, for example, the integrated cycle schedule, regular cycle times, a simple and customer-friendly tariff model and, and, and. There is a whole range of possibilities that have already been implemented in individual cases. Ladies and gentlemen! We know that many people have to be mobile because their homes and their workplaces are located in different places. There are around 512 000 ways to work in Upper Austria, which have to be laid down daily. We have heard today, in Vienna it is about 180 000 commuters who travel to work every day. Therefore, every effort is necessary and important in this direction. If this law does not meet all the wishes immediately, it is an essential step in the right direction and promotes the further development and modernisation of public transport, but also its maintenance in rural areas with socially compatible pricing.

This safety report shows that in 1997 39 695 accidents involving personal injury injured 51 591 persons and unfortunately also 1 105 people were killed. Compared to 1996, the number of accidents increased by 3.8%, the number of injured by 3.9% and the number of fatalities even increased by 7.6 percent. A longer-term analysis of the number of fatalities in Austria shows that the number of accidents has fallen steadily since the 1970s until about 1995, but has since risen again. Although there has been a slight decline in the number of road fatalities compared with 1997 in 1998, there has been a sharp increase in this year again. Now I'm going to look at the question of the causers. This safety report by the Ministry of the Interior shows the following picture in 1997 in the event of fatal road accidents: They were caused by 67 percent of passenger drivers, 9.7 percent of motorbike drivers, 8 percent of truck drivers – it is always overestimated by the risks posed by trucks, because the accidents are usually much more spectacular and are therefore reported – to 4.7 percent of cyclists, to 4.3 percent of pedestrians and to 3.7 percent of mopeds. This means that the hilly road network – which shows the accident situation – is much safer than federal and state roads. The real causes of the fatal road accidents in 1997 were 42 percent high speed, 33 percent high priority injuries, overwhelming, carelessness, 8 percent alcohol consumption and only 1.1 percent technical disease. This means that about 99 percent of accidents are caused by human failure. The technique itself is very erased in the meantime. In particular, we need to look for solutions where the most common causes of accidents are present. That is why I cannot understand why Mrs Gabriela Moser made the request to extend the weekend ban on truck drivers by 5 a.m. on Monday. This would lead to an increased accident frequency on Monday morning. Otherwise, the truck drivers leave at night, at a time when relatively little traffic is available, and thus do not meet with labour traffic in the morning. So you have to think about how to prevent accidents and increase road safety. There must be a gradual elimination of the real causes of the risk of accidents, and there must be no alibi measures. ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e14988	Thank you, honourable Member. I also wish you every good from the Bureau. I call Mr Zweytick. Five minutes. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e15013	Mr President. Estimated Minister of State! High house! I am referring today to the amendment of the Basel Convention on the control of transboundary shipments of hazardous waste and its disposal. On the part of the G-77 States, the preparatory negotiations on the Basel Convention called for a complete ban on exports of hazardous waste from the industrialized countries to the third world. The present amendment to the Convention will now implement this export ban. In the Annex, the Convention is extended by two lists which show which waste is in principle covered by the export ban and which are not. There has been substantial progress here, but of course these lists need to be further edited and extended in the future. This takes into account the demand of G 77 for a comprehensive ban on shipments of waste to developing countries. [[Abg. May. Schweitzer: That's what's in the report!]] Right. I'm referring to that. Mr Schweitzer was also in the committee and knows very well. The European Community has already partially preceded the current change in January 1997 with the amendment of the EC transit regulation, which is why EU conformity is given. This amendment to the Basel Convention is complementary to legislation, which is why it requires approval by the National Council. However, it does not contain any constitutional provisions and has no political character. This amendment, which is also called Ban Amendment, is based on the need for developing countries to be protected from unwanted imports. These countries too often lack the financial, technical, legal and institutional conditions to monitor cross-border shipments of hazardous waste and to prevent illegal imports. The purpose of the ban on exports is to eliminate the weakness of national bans on imports and to encourage the prevention of hazardous waste at the point of origin. Apart from that, the high risk of non-environmental disposal is always associated with the cross-border shipment of hazardous waste. But this Basler Convention must also be lived and observed. The obligation to provide information and examination has its own mechanism, the so-called non-compliance clause, initiated and established by our Environment Minister Martin Bartenstein. [[Abg. May. Schweitzer: What?]] Yeah, you should know, and I think you know. You just have to say it. [[Abg. May. Schweitzer: What did he do? I didn't understand!]] For this reason, ladies and gentlemen, we welcome the ÖVP of this agreement. We see it as an important and absolutely necessary instrument for protecting third world countries from the import of hazardous waste. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e15754	Mr President. Mrs. Federal Minister! Mr. Federal Minister! High house! Under the agenda items 10 to 12 we discuss three laws dealing with the legal trade in the economy and having very strong consumer protection aspects. With regard to signature law and electronic signature – something that is difficult to imagine – I would like to say that if you know what it is for, you will get a relatively clear view that we will not be avoided in the future for an electronic signature. This law is intended to provide clarity about the identity of the contracting party in electronic transport by means of a so-called certificate. That is, if I have over the Internet, over this electronic medium, a contracting partner who is subscribing to me, then I must be sure that the one who subscribing there is also the one I assume it is. This is done by this certificate. It is intended to create a guarantee of trust. This protection of trust is offered through certification services to which you can then rely. In order to ensure that the signature is not used unauthorizedly and cannot be changed unauthorizedly. The full recognition of electronic signatures, of course, requires appropriate organisational, infrastructure, personnel and technical conditions, which we create here very exemplaryly in the international context. There is nothing we can do about this with others, since the European directive is not yet in place. However, it is expected for the beginning of 2000. The need for European equality is obvious, because electronic commerce is, of course, necessary all over the world and is being achieved across all borders, but at least Europe should have the same standards with regard to electronic signature. We are actually giving the standards here, on this question the world is looking at us. [[Complementary of the Abg. Tichy Schreiter.]] The distance selling law, agenda item 10, is also about consumer protection. We are implementing an EU directive which must be implemented by August 2000. It is about the fact that, even in electronic traffic, when it comes to distance transactions with consumers, infringements of laws can be detected even if they are committed abroad. Therefore, this law allows the rightful person to bring actions abroad. Comparative advertising is extended, but it must not under any circumstances be misleading, i.e. comparative advertising itself is not generally negative, as competition is and serves to inform consumers. Furthermore, a right of withdrawal is enshrined in the distance selling law, i.e. even if it concerns business abroad, there is a right of withdrawal, which we have created in an analogy to the Consumer Protection Act; this Seven-Days-Frist is something that applies equally everywhere. Fortunately, a provision on the misuse of credit cards has also been included. In the future, the consumer will have the opportunity to request a refund from the credit card company in the event of fraudulent use of its credit card. You all know, there are always spectacular cases when someone is surprised about his account extract because his credit card was misused somewhere. On the other hand, we have now created a possibility. It is only one way – not one hundred percent protection – but there is now the possibility that the injured person will be better able to come to his right. For the first time, under a Consumer Protection Act, the Council of Seniors has been granted an association clause. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! In my opinion, this is significant in that it is the first time we have given additional rights to the group of seniors, who are becoming increasingly important, not just because they have been understood in numbers, but because their economic significance cannot be underestimated. After all, seniors are among the most powerful in our society, so they have already become an intensive part of our economy. For the first time, this "union clause" gives additional rights to this group's representation of interests, i.e. if the seniors believe that consumer protection infringements have occurred. Seniors in particular are to be protected from abuses or unfair business methods. I think it is justified that this group of stakeholders should be given the importance which it attaches to society as well. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and representatives of the SPÖ.]] Let me say one more word about the item on the agenda 11 which we are dealing with here under one, namely to restrict the time-limit by the 31st. ==References== The question is currently in all mouths: How will the Millennium Spring 2000 be? – It is now true that the 31st. ==References====External links== The ECOFIN Council wished for the banking world or decided to allow the banks a kind of banking day on this Friday, so that they could have a little longer time to make the changeover to the year 2000, until Monday, 3rd. January 2000. Therefore, the Committee on Justice has been asked to establish a corresponding banking system. However, the Committee on Justice has said that this would be a privilege for a single sector. This problem concerns not only the banking world, but also other sectors in our economic life. For this reason, we have established a general rule, namely that if a Sunday or a holiday inhibits a period of time – it is often the case in our legal order that Sunday and holiday regulations are a barrier and it is only the next working day that goes away – this regulation is, in the first place, also by law for this 31st working day. December 1999, which is a Friday, is to apply. We already know a similar rule in our legal order for the Car Friday. The Good Friday is one of the highest holidays for some groups of people; for the Protestants, for example. That is why we took a bond on this example, according to which this Friday, which is not a day of charity, is the 31st anniversary. December 1999, i.e. the last day of this millennium, represents a barrier to time limits. If an expiry date is given on that day, then no legal order will likely be associated with it if only on Monday 3rd. January 2000, which will be put into practice. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e15852	Mr President. Mrs. Federal Minister! Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! One can essentially follow what Mrs Fekter mentioned earlier. It is indeed true that a development in social communication means, of course, also a need to create appropriate consumer protection schemes, appropriate new opportunities under the legal system for dealing with these new requirements. On the one hand, we have to decide today on the distance selling law, which is essentially a implementation of the distance selling directive, but on the other hand we also have to adopt the signature law. What is in store for the electronic commerce is another act, which will then be cast in more precise forms in autumn. The benefits of the implementation of the distance selling directive have in itself been suggested, and above all, it is an improvement in consumer protection in distance transactions. But it has not only been dealt with by the distance sales, but also by a number of other materials which give additional meaning to the law. For example, in the field of injunctions, as mentioned today, it is now possible for the Council of Seniors, in the interest of its members, in the interest of individual seniors – in order to reduce their risk – to conduct processes in particularly important matters in order to clarify important legal issues; on the other hand, within Europe, the competence – and the enforceability of claims by injunctions – has now been guaranteed by consumer protection organisations. This is done in the form of a list of the associations authorised in the respective countries, and these associations can then also implement their claims or claims in the other countries of the European Union cross-border. This is certainly an important improvement, since we have been faced more recently in the field of consumer protection with the fact that legal transactions have been initiated and carried out from outside our borders, whose fights in the domestic field, i.e. in the exercise of legal protection, have been repeated. This is certainly clearly improved in the future. To § 31a Fernätz-Law: In the event of payment card abuse, the credit card holder should in future be better provided, given the need to improve the legal status of the cardholder in view of the fact that a significant expansion of the credit card is taking place, particularly in the area of credit cards. With regard to the associations, I should like to point out that, at the time of the consumer protection and women's ministers, there was a trial against the animal welfare plant, which ended with the finding that there is no right of withdrawal in the case of trades with associations for club members. De facto it is that club members of associations who are abused have the only right to pay for them! In the new law there is now also a provision against this abuse, in such a way that members of the association are granted rights of withdrawal, which is certainly a further improvement. With regard to product liability, it should be noted that agricultural products will also be subject to full product liability in the future. This too, I believe, is a significant step and an improvement for consumers, who will also raise the awareness of producers – in this case farmers – to take care of what is being brought into circulation. On the other hand, it was also not possible to see why there should be an exception in agriculture – and this was ultimately the case in the past – which does not apply to everyone else. In summary, the proposal represents a significant improvement in the current standard, which is carried out by the European Union on the basis of existing requirements. It is certainly a real step, and it will be very important how electronic commerce will be handled in the future. There have been a number of proposals for improvement which we have put in place in the framework of the legislative procedure, but which have not finally been implemented in the coalition debate. It should, for example, be possible for loan contracts to be made possible under the electronic signature and for other contracts such as housing ownership contracts to be concluded with electronic signature, but it should be said that there are a number of cases where the new quality, namely signature in a simplified way, is also in another business context, in another environment, namely at home in front of the computer, but a certain temptation is to conclude contracts that would not be concluded today under existing conditions. It is important to ensure that in the future, too, care is taken to identify where the protection of consumers is required to introduce further measures towards even better consumer protection. All in all, I think it's a good law. However, it is necessary to continue to discuss all these materials intensively in autumn. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e16176	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! The joint consultation of these two draft laws is by no means approximate, but it is based on a uniform approach, on a uniform cross-border approach. Both the distance selling law and the signature law are primarily concerned with creating a legal framework for our information society, which provides security, trust and transparency on the Internet and in other electronic services. The aim of the distance selling law is to ensure that there are clear and clear conditions for consumers and suppliers in traditional shipping and in modern electronic distribution channels, which provide a secure basis for distance transactions. Security, trust and transparency are also the highest ceilings of the Signature Act, as both providers and demanders should be given the opportunity to safely identify their respective contact and to discover any abuses. The creation of such a legal framework is absolutely important, because there are economically important legal issues in e-commerce at present. However, a legal vacuum existing in some areas constitutes a significant obstacle to investment for some providers. The abuse potential, which the Internet and other modern electronic services now offer, also prevents many companies from using the new media and their opportunities accordingly. Consumers who enter these media face difficulties and problems that they can hardly see and are often delivered to. It is therefore all the more important and important to provide for legal provisions that prevent abuse, establish legal clarity and ultimately ensure adequate balance between the different users. However, they are also an important tool for promoting the economic location and thus for ensuring and developing jobs. The rules on distance sales and signature are not limited to Austrian national needs, but also to cross-border transactions. A great deal of attention is paid to legal and economic relations in the internal market. However, it is also not neglected that electronic technologies go beyond Europe and allow cross-border transactions worldwide. Such an international approach is necessary, so to speak, both in the distance selling law and in the signature law, so to speak, so that consumers and businesses can actually benefit from the advantages offered. The Austrian consumers can choose the best offers from a huge market. The Austrian companies offer the international orientation of the Austrian legal framework the opportunity to offer their offers to both men and women worldwide. Both documents originate in EC law – yes, Mr Dkfm. [[Intrusion of the Abg. Dkfm. Holger Bauer]] Bauer – but they are not limited to simply writing down directives or draft directives – in the Signature Act this is even a common position – but rather both projects take into account the specificities and characteristics of the Austrian legal system and take into account specific Austrian needs. In particular, this can be demonstrated by the rules on the so-called competitions, which stop certain abuses which have occurred in Austria in particular. For example, the regulation of credit card abuse in Austria will not only apply to consumer transactions, but will be of general application. I would also like to stress that both the distance selling law and the signature law will further strengthen the position of consumers. Once again, in the Federal Ministry of Justice, we have tried to meet the concerns of Austrian consumers and improve their economic and legal position in an increasingly changing environment. In the end, the government documents or draft legislation to be debated are only a first step in the creation of the necessary legal framework for e-commerce. In order to fully exploit the opportunities and potential of modern technologies, further steps will have to be taken, whether in the field of contract and liability law, whether in criminal law or in administrative law. In order not to be misunderstood: This is not about excessive regulatoryism. It is rather a question of complying with and safeguarding the principles of law in the electronic world. This is important for all users, for businesses as well as for consumers. A strict and uniform legal framework for the information society lies in the legal, economic and consumer interests of all parties involved. It seems to me – and I am particularly pleased – that there is consensus across the party borders. – Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e17112	Mrs Fekter has also been mentioned. Voluntary speaking time limit: 8 minutes. Please, ladies and gentlemen.
ParlaMint-AT_1999-07-14-020-XX-NRSITZ-00180_d7e17134	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! I'm going to deal with the share acquisition law. This is an investor protection law, a creditor protection law that approaches the Austrian financial centre and the Austrian capital market to international standards, as has already been done by the Takeover Act. I feel that an Austrian bank, a very large Austrian bank, has already taken decisions on this subject in anticipation of this law, although the law has not yet been in legal force, namely that 10 per cent of the basic capital can be repurchased without a very specific purpose. Excluded are such buybacks, of course, if they are intended to operate pure speculation or if there is a continuous price influence. We have also included in this law a provision on Stock Options – in other materials we have not yet said anything specific about Stock Options – namely that Stock Options should be subject to special publicity rules. That is, if shares are bought back to offer management as an option, then this should also be announced to investors and creditors. As has to be said, a regulation by the Minister of Justice is to be laid down. The other publicity requirements for Stock Options are not regulated anywhere. There are, of course, rules. In the banking sector in particular, such instruments are strictly regulated by the Compliance Code; even in listed companies, this is covered by the Compliance Code, which probably even acts at least as much as a law. However, I do not want to hide that, if the Vienna financial centre wants to rise, it will not be possible to develop a very open and strict culture of publicity which is not always satisfactory in Austria. Thank you.
